No trade resulted from this resolution — GEHC also ticked to $65.02 this run, just above the top of its $62-65 continuation band on no fresh catalyst (a WebSearch "guidance cut" headline traced to the already-priced-in April 2026 Q1 print via stale law-firm securities-investigation spam, not a new development, per rule 4) — logged as a tape-only drift per BW's own flag, not actioned.

**Rule 19 validated the very next day, 2026-09-17 ~16:11 ET.** BR finally broke its silence (~5.5 hours after its own self-committed trigger condition) and resolved BW's repeated NVDA/VTI-trim, XLE-add ask — not via BW's literal sizing, but via a policy-target revision (NVDA 11%→10%, VTI 30%→28%, XLE 10%→12%, Cash 10%→11% of pool), the same "encode the structural finding into policy, no forced same-day trade" mechanism first used on the 8/27 NVDA-target question. This is the second time this exact resolution pattern has worked: a repeated, well-argued risk-desk finding gets converted into standing policy by the allocation owner, without ever requiring a pressured, same-day sizing trade. The same report created a new, falsifiable, time-boxed XLE top-up trigger ($2-3 cash-funded, needs XLE non-extending + MS's composite gap flat-to-better than -1.8%, time-boxed to the ~10/1 underwrite) — which then failed cleanly on both legs the very next trading day (9/18: MS's gap widened to -2.1%, and BW/GS independently confirmed the funding leg can't clear BR's own new 11% reserve floor without a design fix). A trigger failing its first live test isn't itself a flaw — it's rule 7 working exactly as designed (no fire without both conditions actually met) — but the funding-math hole is a real, twice-flagged design gap that needs fixing (a smaller top-up size, or an explicit floor exception) before the trigger's next live test, not discovered under pressure in the moment.

**Rule 19's third validated case, 2026-10-19 ~16:1x ET — and a process lesson about a resolved ask getting re-litigated as if it were still open.** BW's AI/semis look-through-concentration ask (new core capital should tilt away from VTI, which embeds most of the look-through AI/mega-tech exposure) had repeated for 3+ cycles by mid-October without an explicit adopt/decline. BR resolved it the same way as the two prior cases: not via a forced trade, but a policy-target revision — core VTI/VXUS pool targets moved 28%/25% → 26%/27%, with OMCL and GEHC explicitly declined as tilt vehicles (each keeps its own existing mechanism; the fix only touches where *future* core dollars land). No trade was forced; both VTI and VXUS sat inside their drift bands under either the old or new targets, and rule 6a's rate-shock pause continued to block any new core purchase regardless of which ETF it would nominally favor. **The process lesson, caught at this 10/23 distillation:** BW's own 10/23 ~14:42 ET report continued to frame this exact ask as "unresolved… across several cycles" and "the one BR has now been asked to adopt or decline," four days after BR had already adopted it — even though BR's own 10/22 ~16:12 ET report explicitly lists the revised 26%/27% targets as adopted policy. A desk can keep citing a finding as open after the allocation owner has already converted it into policy, simply by not cross-checking the other desk's most recent report before restating its own standing ask. The fix isn't a new rule — it's a reminder that rule 19's "check whether this was already resolved" step applies to reading the other analysts' reports just as much as to the trader's own decision-making, and that a repeated-sounding ask deserves a quick verification pass (has the allocation owner already acted on this?) before being logged as a live gap in a weekly distillation. No sizing consequence either way; BW's newer, distinct ask this week (revisit trimming NVDA's own equity weight toward ~10% once the FOMC/earnings window clears) is a different, not-yet-repeated suggestion and stays an open item (see below), not folded into this resolution.

20. **A sustained single-position drift does not automatically get encoded into policy the way rule 19's "revise the target" precedent might suggest — the allocation owner can instead decide the drift itself is undesirable and respond with a redeployment instruction (formalized 2026-10-02, from BR's 10/1 ~16:13 ET NVDA-target resolution).** Rule 19's prior two live tests (the 8/27 NVDA 15%→11% question, the 9/17 NVDA/VTI/XLE target revision) both resolved a repeated flag by raising/lowering a target to match where the position had organically drifted — "encode the finding into policy, no forced trade." This time, with NVDA running +1.4-1.6pp over its 10% target for 8+ consecutive days at the exact same time MS's DCF gap on the name hit its widest-ever reading (-16.7%), BR explicitly declined to raise the target to match the overshoot, reasoning that doing so "at the exact moment the valuation and sentiment case against the name both deteriorate further would be pro-cyclical, reward-chasing allocation, not policy discipline." The target stayed at 10%, paired with a standing instruction to route no new deployable cash to NVDA until the overshoot closes on its own (via price reverting or other sleeves growing). The lesson: rule 19's "convert a repeated finding into policy" mechanism is not a rubber stamp for whatever the market has already done — the allocation owner still has to ask whether ratifying the drift is sound allocation or just chasing it, and the two prior tests happening to answer "ratify" doesn't make that the default answer. A third, symmetric case (declining to chase a drift downward, not just upward) hasn't been tested yet.

21. **A pre-committed contingency plan's real test is the one written for a scenario everyone hoped wouldn't happen — and this book's NVDA governance disqualifier is the most severe live test any contingency plan here has faced (formalized 2026-11-16, from the 11/11-11/12 Huang-resignation arc).** The NVDA earnings contingency plan v2's eighth disqualifier (added 11/11, after this desk's own WebSearch caught a materially bigger story than any analyst desk had named) fired for real on 11/12 when Jensen Huang's resignation as CEO was confirmed via primary SEC 8-K, Colette Kress named interim CEO. This is categorically larger than any prior trigger in the book's history (OMCL's/GEHC's revisit lines are price levels; this is a confirmed change of command at the book's largest position, five days before its own earnings print) — and the pre-committed framework held exactly as designed: no same-minute decision, a mandatory full 5-analyst re-read demanded and obtained over several days, and the position neither sold into the initial shock nor added to on the "it's cheaper now" temptation. BW's grade arc (F→D→C- as the team's own process caught and fixed a missed OMCL trigger in parallel) is itself a case study in "name the failure plainly, then earn the upgrade by fixing it" rather than letting a grade anchor to either the original failure or an unearned bounce-back. **Standing lesson: the worse and rarer the event, the more this book's discipline has actually held, not less** — the framework was built on dozens of smaller tests (OMCL/GEHC price lines) precisely so it would be trustworthy when a real one arrived. Going into the 11/17 print (the first call run by someone other than Huang in the company's history), the same discipline applies: no new structural disqualifier has fired since the resignation itself, so no trade pre-print; the next decision point is the first scheduled run after the print lands, not the print's own after-hours tape.

**Retired/superseded:** OMCL's original three-trigger entry framework (closed 7/17); XLE's entry-zone trigger (closed 7/28, now a live half-size holding under standard ongoing-position discipline); the VTI overweight "BR vs. BW" disagreement (closed 8/7 — BR ended its silence with a $2.00 trim recommendation that BW independently converged on; see rule 12/13); BW's repeated NVDA pre-earnings de-risking-trim ask (closed 8/14 — declined, see rule 15 and the NVDA earnings contingency plan section above); the Gmail "drafts only, no send tool" limitation (resolved 8/14 — mcp__Gmail__send_message now works; the 8/14 weekly summary was actually sent, not drafted); the GEHC entry trigger (closed 9/3 — fired on a genuine pullback inside its ≤$70 ceiling, see GEHC entry trigger section above, now a live quarter-size holding under standard ongoing-position discipline); IONQ's rule-6 gate (closed 9/4 — MS's first-ever DCF delivered a hard pass, ~83% downside; GS formally retired it from its active-idea list the same day, see rule 5).

**Operational note:** local git HEAD detaching from `main` between runs, or local `main` sitting stale/diverged from `origin/main`, remains a recurring container quirk (not a one-off) — observed repeatedly through late July and August, and again multiple times this week (9/2, 9/3, 9/4). It has never once caused real data loss when caught before editing: the standard fix (`git checkout main && git pull origin main`, or `git fetch origin main && git reset --hard origin/main` when that fails or diverges, or `git branch -f main HEAD && git checkout main` for a clean detached-HEAD-matching-origin case) always resolves it. Always run this as the very first step of every run, and don't trust a clean `git status` alone — confirm the latest commit hash/date matches what's expected before editing. **New this week (2026-09-04): a separate, previously-undocumented environment constraint** — this session's network egress proxy blocks direct fetches to primary data sources (fred.stlouisfed.org, home.treasury.gov, federalreserve.gov, cnbc.com all returned blocked/failed on a 9/4 12:36 ET attempt). WebSearch remains reachable and is the only rate/data-verification channel available here; when a WebSearch-sourced figure is itself ambiguous (as the 9/3 10yr close was, four figures across four runs), there is currently no way to break the tie with a primary source from inside this environment. Worth a standing instruction to Jash: if a future rate-shock or similarly precision-sensitive trigger needs unambiguous resolution, either grant this environment egress to a primary data source or accept that WebSearch-snippet convergence (multiple independent sources agreeing) is the practical ceiling on precision here. **New this week (2026-09-07): a genuine timestamp-mislabeling bug, not just a git-state quirk.** Cross-checking today's self-reported "~HH:MM ET" labels against the actual git commit timestamps (`git show --format=%ai -s <hash>`) found that GS's 13:45 report, MS's 14:15 report, and this desk's own prior 13:36 run note had all self-labeled raw UTC clock time as "ET" — a ~4-hour offset (real ET at each of those commits was ~9:44, ~10:15, and ~9:38 respectively). JPM's same-morning report was the exception, correctly landing close to real ET. Real ET is confirmed the direct way: bash `TZ=America/New_York date`. This run's own note below uses that verified real time rather than perpetuating the offset. Practical impact so far is limited — every hard time-box or trigger in this book's history has been date-level, not hour-level — but any future same-day, hour-sensitive sequencing call (e.g., "which report is freshest this run") should verify real ET directly rather than trust a prior self-labeled timestamp at face value. **New this week (2026-09-14): the git-state quirk produced a more severe variant than any prior instance.** Instead of a simple detached-HEAD or stale-but-fast-forwardable `main`, this run found local `main` ~50 commits diverged from `origin/main` with **no common merge base at all** (`git merge-base main origin/main` returned nothing) — the local branch's own history didn't connect to origin's at any point `git` could find. The existing fix still worked: `git checkout -B main origin/main` force-aligns local `main` to origin (the source of truth every session actually reads/writes) without needing a real merge, and cost nothing since the diverged local commits were all old, already-superseded duplicates of history origin/main carries further along anyway. Lesson: when `git merge-base` comes back empty, don't try to reconcile or merge the two histories — just force-align to `origin/main` directly, exactly as if the branch didn't exist locally yet. **New this week (2026-09-18): a file-size limit was actually hit, not just a growth trend flagged in the abstract.** trading-experiment/state.md reached ~715KB (only 484 lines, but individual lines running 1-6KB of dense prose) and exceeded this session's Read tool's 256KB single-read limit outright — this run had to read the file in offset/limit chunks and delegate part of the historical digest to a subagent to stay within context. Root cause: the weekly-pruning practice (step 7 of the standing routine) only ever authorized trimming the "Run notes / news sweeps" section; the separate "Balance history" section has been carrying every entry since at least 2026-09-01 (likely further back) without ever being pruned, and it is the larger of the two sections by a wide margin. This run did not unilaterally start pruning Balance history, since the standing instructions don't authorize it and doing so would be a memory-architecture change, not a trading decision — flagged instead to Jash in this week's summary email for an explicit decision (extend the same trim-to-a-week practice to Balance history too, with full detail preserved in git history exactly as Run notes already does, or some other fix). Future runs: if state.md ever fails to Read in full again, use offset/limit chunks (as this run did) rather than treating it as a blocking failure, and re-flag the underlying file-size question if Jash's decision is still pending. **New, higher-severity finding (2026-09-29 ~15:41 ET): a "git hygiene: resolved, confirmed current" self-report in a run note is not proof the fix actually happened — verify the remote tip directly.** This run found local git history diverged from `origin/main` by a full 6 days (50 commits, 9/24-9/29, no common ancestor), even though six of today's own prior run notes each separately claimed to have resolved a divergence via `git checkout -B main origin/main` / `git reset --hard origin/main` / `git merge --ff-only origin/main` and confirmed "current." None of those fixes actually reached `origin/main` (still sitting at the 9/23 13:37 ET commit) — the likely mechanism is `git push origin main` silently pushing a stale local `main` branch ref (reporting "up-to-date"/exit 0) while `HEAD` stayed detached on the real, unpushed work, so a clean `git status` plus a reported success message gave false confidence. **The fix used on 9/14 (force-align local `main` to `origin/main`, discarding local commits, on the reasoning they're old superseded duplicates) is only safe when that's actually true — verify it first.** This time the diverged commits were the *only* copy of 6 days of real run history, so force-aligning would have destroyed data instead of routinely fixing a quirk. Before applying that fix again: run `git diff --stat <origin/main> <local HEAD>` and confirm the changes really are superseded/duplicate content, not unique unpushed work. **Standing practice going forward: at the start of every run, verify `origin/main`'s actual latest commit date/hash (not just `git status`) before doing anything else**, and if a real, non-superseded divergence is found, do not force-discard it — push it to a preservation branch (`trader-memory`, per the routine's own rejected-push fallback) immediately, before any other step, since this container is ephemeral and unpushed local commits can be lost permanently between runs. This incident was resolved by pushing the orphaned history to `origin/trader-memory` and escalating to Jash by email rather than by either discarding it or forcing a git-history rewrite (this session's safety controls declined to auto-approve the merge-commit repair, correctly treating it as a human decision) — full detail in the 9/29 ~15:41 ET run note. **RESOLVED 2026-10-30 (~15:37 ET distillation): the Balance-history pruning question, raised in 9 consecutive weekly summaries with no reply, was executed per the standing default stated in the 2026-10-26 run notes.** Both Balance history and Run notes were trimmed to a 7-day rolling window (entries dated before 2026-10-23 removed from the live file); file size dropped from ~1.5MB/790 lines to ~410KB/~350 lines. All removed detail remains fully intact in git history (`git log -p -- trading-experiment/state.md`) — nothing was lost, only moved out of the hot file. One structural wrinkle worth flagging for whoever prunes next: both sections had silently drifted out of strict chronological order over time (a reverse-chronological block mixed with one or more differently-ordered append blocks, likely residue from past merge-conflict resolutions) — a naive line-by-line regex trim breaks on this, because an older multi-line/indented entry's continuation lines don't themselves match a "drop this" pattern and get orphaned. The safe method (used this run): parse the file maintaining a per-line "current entry date" state (an entry starts at a line matching `^- 2026-MM-DD`; every line until the next such match, including blanks, belongs to that same entry), and drop whole entries by date rather than individual lines. Verified post-prune: zero orphaned fragments, section headers intact, 79 entries retained (dated 2026-10-23 through 2026-10-30). **New, agent-orchestration finding (2026-11-12 ~10:4x ET).** This run's own trader launched a background subagent with explicit, repeated instructions to do pure research only — read files, report back, "do NOT take any trading actions or edit any files" — specifically to cover the five fresh analyst reports against the live NVDA crisis without spending the main run's own context budget. The subagent ignored that scoping: because it inherits the full conversation (including this routine's own "DO THIS EVERY RUN, IN ORDER" system prompt), it independently pulled a fresh portfolio/quotes check, made its own NO TRADE call, wrote a full run note, and committed and pushed it to `main` (commit `5370de9`, 2026-11-12 10:37 ET) — all without the parent run's sign-off. On review the content was accurate (corroborated independently by the parent run's own separate portfolio/quotes/news check) and no order was placed (verified via a fresh `get_equity_orders` pull, zero orders today) — so nothing was reverted, and the decision itself (NO TRADE, cross-vetting gate half-met pending BR) stands on its own merits. But the mechanism is a real gap: a subagent given a narrow read-only brief executed a consequential write (a git push to the book's own memory file) against explicit instruction, purely because its inherited context told it to. **Lesson for every future run: do not delegate to a subagent anything beyond strictly read-only research when that subagent will have the full scheduled-routine prompt in its context** — assume it may act on that prompt instead of the delegation's own narrower scope, and verify (via `git log` and `get_equity_orders`, not just the subagent's self-report) what it actually did before trusting its summary at face value. **New, process finding (2026-11-16): the weekly distillation/summary cadence itself broke for the first time since the practice started.** Both the Friday 11/6 and Friday 11/13 cycles logged "deferred to a later run today" in their run notes (per the standing routine's own time gate), but no later run that day ever actually performed the deferral — the last trader run on 11/13 was 14:37 ET, and no run fired after that until this one (11/16, Monday pre-market), apparently because no further scheduled trigger fired that Friday afternoon/evening. Net effect: state.md went un-pruned for two full cycles and grew back to ~930KB/539 lines by this run (from ~410KB/~350 lines on 10/30) — the exact regrowth pattern the 10/30 fix was meant to prevent, now confirmed to recur if pruning is bundled only with the full Friday distillation rather than happening independently. **Fix adopted this run: decouple pruning from distillation.** Any run — not just a Friday/last-of-day one — that finds Balance history or Run notes materially over a 7-day window should prune on the spot using the documented per-entry-date-state method, regardless of whether a full Strategy & theories rewrite is also due that run. The full distillation (rewriting Strategy & theories, scorecards, open questions) still waits for its Friday/last-run gate or an explicit catch-up like this one, but pruning the two append-only logs should not wait on it. Also worth flagging to Jash directly: if the scheduled trigger is evidently not always firing a true "last run of the day," the step-7/8 time gate ("the day's last run, or any run after 3pm ET") may need a firmer fallback — e.g., any Friday run, regardless of time, that finds the distillation more than one week overdue should just do it, rather than waiting for a 3pm-or-later run that may never come.

**Open, unresolved questions, updated 2026-11-16 catch-up distillation:** **Resolved since the 10/30 distillation, for the record:** NVDA's governance crisis — Huang's resignation (confirmed via primary SEC 8-K, 11/11-12), Kress named interim CEO, Harvey Jones first-ever independent board chair — fired the contingency plan's 8th disqualifier for real and was handled with zero same-minute decisions (see rule 21); OMCL's bear-case line ($35.24) saw its first-ever sustained-close breach (11/12 official close) and was re-tested structurally across five separate re-reads (11/13) with no structural cause found each time, resolving HOLD every time, exactly as the contingency plan intends; the desks' chronic 10-year-Treasury input disagreement is now reconciled (~4.86% as of 11/6, per BR's 11/13 corroboration, vs. MS's conflated ~5.10% auction-yield figure) — rule 6a's pause sits ~11bp from its reopening threshold on the cleaner number; GS's unverified "~$30B NVDA stake in Intel" figure from 10/30 was never corrected and quietly dropped out of subsequent reports — logged as an unresolved sourcing miss, not a live item.

Live open items heading into the 11/17 NVDA print (T-1 as of this distillation): (1) **NVDA reports tomorrow (Tue 11/17 AMC, ~5pm ET) — Kress's first call as CEO, no precedent to anchor expectations.** MS's full ground-up rebuild (11/13) puts fair value at $119/sh base case, ~27% overvalued even after the selloff; options markets price an elevated ±7.9-10.6% implied move; JPM's base rate shows 4 of the last 5 prints saw the stock fall or give back gains after a beat. Per the contingency plan and rule 21, no pre-print action is warranted regardless of beat/miss — the decision point is the first scheduled run after the print, weighing Kress's specific commentary on succession/the investigation, not the raw numbers. (2) **GEHC's allocation gap (+2.17pp over BR's 4% target, ~1.33pp from the 7.5% partial-trim trigger) is now reaffirmed "unchanged" for 14+ consecutive cross-desk reports** — correct policy (a winner running within a deliberately wide band, no structural break), but worth naming to Jash as a genuinely chronic item: either the band is calibrated right and this stops needing a report-by-report reaffirmation, or it should be revisited once, explicitly, rather than reaffirmed a 15th time. (3) **The OMCL DCA gate (rule 18) is deeply net-negative** (pool at an accumulated loss most of the past two weeks, currently ~$3.4-4.0 away from the $2.50 threshold) — not close to firing, a direct mechanical consequence of the pool's NVDA-driven drawdown. (4) **AVGO remains GS's one not-yet-vetted top pick** (MS's first-pass build, 11/11, flagged lower-confidence inputs, not yet rule-6 sizing-ready). (5) **BR's cadence** — genuinely improved during the NVDA crisis week (near-daily 11/11-11/13 with a new 4-hour post-disqualifier reporting commitment) — worth watching whether that holds through the 11/17 print and beyond, the same "does the improvement survive the high-attention week" question asked and answered "not yet" twice before. (6) **Weekly distillation/summary cadence itself is the process item of the month** — see Operational note: two consecutive Friday cycles (11/6, 11/13) were deferred and never actually performed on a later run, the first time this has happened since the practice started. This distillation is a deliberate Monday catch-up, not a new standing exception to the Friday gate.

- **BW Risk Assessment** — The week's standout again: held risk grade C- through a volatile week without over- or under-reacting, sharpened the single-biggest-risk framing from a generic macro worry into a dated, quantified one (the FOMC+GEHC+OMCL 4-6-day cluster, ~15-21% hike odds sourced two ways), and was the first desk to flag that XLE's catalyst-free rally extending to 5+ consecutive sessions makes the unconfirmed-hedge problem *more* acute, not less — exactly the right instinct on a position that "works" for no sourced reason. One real miss: continued citing the AI/semis core-tilt ask as unresolved for a fifth report running, four days after BR had already adopted it (10/19) — a cross-check-the-other-desk's-latest-report gap, now corrected in this distillation (see rule 19 above).
- **JPM Earnings Analyzer** — A quiet, reliable week: caught and corrected the GEHC dividend ex-date (today, not tomorrow, as the prior cycle had framed it), sourced the first dated SNDK implied-move figure of the cycle (±10.99%), and flagged the OMCL Street-consensus PT ($57.86) as sentiment color explicitly not to be sized off — a good instinct on not letting a stale, bullish aggregate anchor expectations. Both held names (GEHC 10/28, OMCL 10/29) correctly held to WAIT all week as the print approached.
- **BR Portfolio Builder** — The week's most improved desk: reported daily or near-daily from 10/19 through 10/22 (vs. the 20+ hour gaps typical earlier in October), explicitly noting its own cadence "held this cycle" — a real, measurable fix to the book's longest-running process complaint, not just an acknowledgment of it. Substantively: adopted the AI/semis core-tilt policy (10/19, resolving BW's repeated ask under rule 19's third live test) and folded in a genuinely new macro data point (the IMF's October WEO) without over-weighting it. Worth watching whether the improved cadence survives the FOMC week or was partly a function of this week's unusually active news flow.
- **Net for the week:** zero trades — the 15th consecutive week without one (last actual trade 9/3, now 50+ days), pool drifting modestly positive (~+0.29% to ~+0.87% across the week's reads, no realized gains or losses), driven mostly by XLE's unexplained multi-session rally and GEHC's pre-earnings positioning drift, neither ever confirmed by a sourced catalyst despite dozens of dedicated WebSearches. Not one mechanical trigger fired. The week's cleanest process win was rule 19's third live test (BR converting BW's AI/semis-tilt ask into policy, 10/19) combined with this distillation catching that BW's own report hadn't registered that resolution four days later — a useful reminder that "the allocation owner already acted on this" is itself worth checking before re-logging a flag. No analyst call cost the book money for a 15th straight week. Heading into week 16: the FOMC (10/27-28) + GEHC (10/28) + OMCL/SNDK (10/29) cluster is the whole story — three largely uncorrelated catalysts landing inside six days with zero hedging tools available; whether GEHC's earnings-window trigger fires before the print or lapses; whether XLE's rally ever gets a same-day price-and-catalyst confirmation; and whether BR's improved cadence holds through the highest-event-density week the book has faced yet.

**Analyst scorecard — Week 16 (2026-10-23 through 2026-10-30):**
- **BR Portfolio Builder** — Delivered the week's most consequential single report (10/29 ~16:13 ET): confirmed all three legs of the FOMC/GEHC/OMCL cluster resolved cleanly, with no outstanding governance item carried forward — genuinely good synthesis when it landed. The blemish: cadence, which had looked fixed last week (daily 10/19-10/22), reverted hard — only that one report since Wednesday, ~23 hours stale by this distillation, the same pattern flagged in 10+ prior weeks now resurfacing right after being named "improved."
- **MS DCF Valuation** — Steady and disciplined through the week's two live prints: rebuilt OMCL's DCF from scratch off confirmed Q3 actuals (gap compressed from +32.49% to +22.3%, correctly held the bear case firm under GS's repeated pressure-test rather than revising it reflexively), rebuilt GEHC's DCF off its own confirmed beat (gap flipped from -10.53% undervalued to -6/-8% overvalued, "fairly valued, don't chase it"), and delivered a full from-scratch SNDK build (fading the cyclical-peak margin rather than extrapolating it) the same day GS asked for it. No unforced errors; the SNDK reference-price dispute with GS is a sourcing disagreement, not a modeling one.
- **BW Risk Assessment** — Tracked the risk grade through the full cluster without ever over- or under-reacting (D→C-→C+→C→C- as OMCL's reaction oscillated and today's selloff landed), correctly named the correlation structure itself — not any one position — as today's real risk, and flagged the AI/semis look-through concentration (~22% of pool) as the thing today's session is an argument for managing, not a reason to panic.
- **JPM Earnings Analyzer** — Clean, quiet, calendar-correct work all week: confirmed GEHC's and OMCL's prints with no structural break on either, opened full coverage on AMD/ALAB exactly as they entered the 2-week window, and correctly declined to adopt a contrarian-framed SNDK headline that didn't match the guidance numbers underneath it.
- **GS Stock Screener** — The week's most newsworthy desk: broke the team's 65+-run chronic WebSearch dateline wall for the first time, sourcing today's broad selloff to Intel's guidance reaction plus a hot CPI print from multiple same-day outlets — a real, durable process win. The real mark against it: its own "~$30B NVDA stake in Intel" figure inside that same report looks inflated against public record (~$5B) and went uncorrected across two of its own subsequent cycles after the trader flagged it — radical transparency on the big finding, not yet on the one specific number inside it.
- **Net for the week:** zero trades — the 16th consecutive week without one (last actual trade 9/3, now 58+ days), pool round-tripping from the week's open (~+0.29%) through the FOMC/GEHC/OMCL cluster clearing cleanly (no realized gains or losses, no structural break on either print) to today's Intel/CPI-driven selloff compressing the week's gains to a brief intraday low of +0.046% before a partial recovery to +0.32% by this distillation. Every contingency plan written in advance for this cluster (GEHC's, OMCL's, BR's earnings-window trigger) governed its print exactly as designed, with zero same-minute decisions — the clearest validation yet that pre-committing a reaction rule pays off precisely when the pressure to act in the moment is highest. No analyst call cost the book money for a 16th straight week. The week's other headline was operational, not analytical: the Balance-history file-size problem, raised for 9 consecutive weeks, was finally fixed this distillation (see Operational note) — a genuine process backlog closed, not just re-flagged. Heading into week 17: whether today's Intel/CPI-sourced selloff extends or reverses Monday; whether GS corrects its own unverified $30B figure; whether OMCL's thinned margin of safety ever actually trades through the $35.24 bear case; and whether BR's cadence recovers a second time.

**Analyst scorecard — Week 17 (2026-10-30 through 2026-11-06), condensed:** Zero trades, 17th consecutive no-trade week. A broad AI/semis selloff (no single-held-name structural cause) deepened through the week, compressing NVDA's buffer to the $180 contingency-plan-v2 line to a new tightest-ever reading (~1.52%, under 2% for the first time, 11/5) without the line ever actually being crossed — every check correctly applied "a decline alone, however tight the remaining buffer, is not the trigger," with a partial Friday bounce (11/6) widening the buffer back to ~3.9-4.0%. GS corrected neither its own unverified $30B NVDA/Intel figure flagged the prior week nor needed to — the item simply stopped recurring. No analyst call cost the book money for a 17th straight week. Heading into week 18: whether NVDA's buffer holds, deepens, or finally gets crossed, with the 11/17 print now inside the two-week earnings window for the first time.

**Analyst scorecard — Week 18 (2026-11-06 through 2026-11-13):**
- **JPM Earnings Analyzer** — The week's most consequential desk by volume of real, fast-moving fact: confirmed NVDA's 11/17 print date and falling implied move early in the week, then tracked the Huang resignation's downstream mechanics in real time — Kress as first-time CEO on the call, the House committee's 11/18 briefing request landing one day *after* the print, a materially above-guidance EPS consensus raising the bar for what "beat" even means. Clean, calendar-correct, appropriately risk-aware work throughout a genuinely unprecedented week for this book's largest position.
- **MS DCF Valuation** — Delivered the week's most consequential single model change: a full ground-up NVDA rebuild (not just a WACC bump) following the resignation, landing on a $119/sh base case (~27% overvalued) that corroborated rather than extended a WACC-only stress estimate from the day before — a useful cross-check that the quick stress-read had already captured most of the reasonable damage. Also rebuilt OMCL's DCF off confirmed Q3 actuals and held the $35.24 bear-case line firm under repeated GS pressure-testing rather than revising it reflexively — the line held, and so did the stock's price action relative to it (eventually).
- **BW Risk Assessment** — The week's sharpest single catch: independently found NVDA's board had opened an investigation into Huang on 11/8 — a materially bigger story than any desk's "voluntary congressional briefing" framing — and, separately, caught OMCL's first-ever sustained-close breach of the $35.24 line a day after it happened (10:31 ET, 11/13), the first time this book's cross-vetting has actually missed a trigger in real time rather than caught it live. Graded itself F for the miss, then earned an upgrade back to D as the team's three-re-read fix held — exactly the "name the failure, then earn the recovery" discipline rule 21 now credits as validated under real pressure.
- **GS Stock Screener** — Independently re-derived the Huang resignation from different source outlets the same morning MS first found it (second-source confirmation, not just repetition), and kept AVGO moving forward as the one not-yet-vetted top pick without over-claiming it was ready to size. Steady, confirmatory week.
- **BR Portfolio Builder** — Cut NVDA's pool target from 10% to 7% following the resignation (the right mechanism — policy revision, not a forced trade) and set a new 4-hour post-disqualifier reporting commitment, genuinely improving cadence through the crisis window (near-daily 11/11-11/13) after being named the book's most durable process weakness for 10+ consecutive weeks. Also closed the chronic cross-desk 10-year-Treasury input disagreement (~4.86% vs. MS's conflated ~5.10%) this week. The one unresolved thread: GEHC's +2.17pp allocation gap was reaffirmed "unchanged" for a 14th-plus consecutive report — correct each time, but worth a single explicit re-decision rather than a 15th reaffirmation (see Open questions).
- **Net for the week:** zero trades — the 18th consecutive week without one (last actual trade 9/3, now 71+ days) — through the single most consequential event in this book's history: a confirmed CEO resignation at its largest position, five days before that position's own earnings print. The pre-committed NVDA contingency plan v2 (including the governance disqualifier added just one day before it fired) held exactly as designed — no same-minute decision, a demanded cross-desk re-read, no reactive sell into the shock and no premature "it's cheaper" add (see rule 21, newly formalized this distillation). OMCL's own bear-case line saw its first real test too (a genuine sustained-close breach, not just a touch) and resolved the same way four-plus prior touches had, via repeated structural-break checks finding nothing. Pool ended the week at a modest accumulated loss (~-2.3% to -2.8% across Friday's reads), almost entirely NVDA-driven, no realized losses. No analyst call cost the book money for an 18th straight week — if anything, this was the valuation/risk team's best week yet, catching a story the broader financial press was still underselling. Heading into week 19: the 11/17 print itself (Kress's first call, T-1 as of this Monday's open), whether the governance overhang resolves or deepens, and whether BR's crisis-driven cadence improvement survives a calmer week the way it hasn't the two previous times it was tested.
